=== PROMPT VARIANT ===
r1

=== PROMPT ===
You will be given a competitive programming problem.
You are given integer sequences of length N: A=(A_1,A_2,\cdots,A_N) and B=(B_1,B_2,\cdots,B_N), and an integer K.
You can perform the following operation zero or more times.

- Choose integers i and j (1 \leq i,j \leq N).
Here, |i-j| \leq K must hold.
Then, change the value of A_i to A_j.

Determine whether it is possible to make A identical to B.
There are T test cases for each input.

Input

The input is given from Standard Input in the following format:
T
case_1
case_2
\vdots
case_T

Each test case is given in the following format:
N K
A_1 A_2 \cdots A_N
B_1 B_2 \cdots B_N

Output

For each test case, print Yes if it is possible to make A identical to B, and No otherwise.

Constraints


- 1 \leq T \leq 125000
- 1 \leq K < N \leq 250000
- 1 \leq A_i,B_i \leq N
- The sum of N across all test cases in each input is at most 250000.
- All input values are integers.

Sample Input 1

4
3 1
1 1 2
1 2 2
5 4
2 4 5 1 3
2 1 3 2 2
13 1
3 1 3 3 5 3 3 4 2 2 2 5 1
5 3 3 3 4 2 2 2 2 5 5 1 3
20 14
10 6 6 19 13 16 15 15 2 10 2 16 9 12 2 6 13 5 5 9
5 9 6 2 10 19 16 15 13 12 10 2 9 6 5 16 19 12 15 13

Sample Output 1

Yes
Yes
No
Yes

Consider the first test case.
If we operate with i=2 and j=3, the value of A_2 will be changed to A_3=2, resulting in A=(1,2,2).

Use the following starter code to write the final solution.
```python

```

Reason in a commit-implement-recheck way.

A productive rhythm for this task usually looks like this:
- Isolate the decisive constraints, the target complexity, and at most one or two approaches that are still truly plausible.
- If two candidates remain, compare them only on the decisive constraint and commit quickly to the stronger one instead of keeping a wide search open.
- Before coding, keep only a tiny implementation ledger for the chosen plan: main invariant, key state or transition, and the one or two edge cases most likely to fail.
- Move into code as soon as that ledger is clear. After coding starts, keep reasoning attached to the current draft rather than reopening the high-level search.
- After the first complete draft, do one short implementation-check-adjust-check loop on the same draft: test the key edge case, patch locally if needed, and recheck.
- Reopen the main design only if a concrete counterexample breaks the core invariant. Otherwise prefer the local patch and keep closing.
- Once the current draft, invariant, and short recheck still agree, finalize the solution.

Try to avoid patterns like these:
- spending too long comparing nearby plans before committing to one workable approach;
- returning from code to broad algorithm discussion without a concrete contradiction;
- turning one local doubt into a full restart when the current draft only needs a small patch.

Output only the final Python code enclosed in a code block.

Alternative perspective: Each operation copies value from j to i. This is like a directed edge j -> i (source to destination) at time. The final value at position i is the value of some initial position p that is an ancestor in a directed forest where edges go from source to destination within distance K. Since a value can be copied multiple times, the ancestry graph is a forest (each position's current value originates from one initial position). The final array B is obtained by assigning each position i to an initial position p(i) such that B_i = A_{p(i)}, and there exists a directed forest/arborescence from p(i) to i using edges of length <=K, with the additional constraint that each initial position can be used as source for many copies, but if a position is overwritten, it can no longer be a source of its original value? Wait if position x initially value u is overwritten by v, then it can no longer serve as source for u, but copies of u made before overwrite can. In ancestry forest, each final position has a chain of copy operations: p = x0 -> x1 -> ... -> xm = i, where each edge x_t -> x_{t+1} is an operation at time t+1 copying value from x_t to x_{t+1}. For this to be valid, at time t+1, x_t must currently have the value (it might have been overwritten later, but at that time it had it). The chain can branch: a position can be source for many destinations at different times. If a position is overwritten, it cannot be source for its old value after that. But if it is overwritten, it might later be source for new value. So each final position's value has a "lineage" through positions that held that value. The lineage is a path in space-time. Since graph is connected, any initial position can reach any final position via path, but need avoid time conflicts? If a position is used as source for value v at time t, it must have v at time t. It could have been set to v earlier by another source. So v spreads as a wave. Multiple waves can cross.

This is similar to "graph search" where each value expands from initial sources; overwriting can reclaim cells. Since values are independent except for cell occupancy. We can think of each value v as a "color" that can spread to adjacent cells; if a cell is colored v, it can be recolored by another color. We need final coloring B. Starting coloring A. This is exactly multi-color graph recoloring by copying adjacent colors. Since colors can spread along edges, any color can reach any cell if there is a path. Let's characterize reverse process more.

Operation: pick edge (u,v) with same color, recolor u arbitrarily. This is equivalent to "a color class with at least one edge can be used to recolor one endpoint; the other endpoint remains as a witness of that color." If after recoloring u, the witness v may lose all same-color neighbors (if u was its only neighbor), then that color class may become edgeless, so no further operations with that color. But we could first create more witnesses by recoloring other vertices to that color using other flexible colors.

This is analogous to having "markers" (flexible edges) that can be moved. A flexible edge of color c at positions u,v allows us to change u to any color d. If d appears at a neighbor w, then u becomes part of a d-edge (u,w), so flexibility moves to d. If d does not appear adjacent, u becomes frozen, and v may remain as a c but with no edge (if no other c neighbor). Thus the flexibility may disappear. To keep flexibility, when recoloring u to d, we should choose d equal to a neighbor of u (other than v? v has c, not d) so that u has a same-color neighbor. Or choose d=c? no change. Thus to propagate flexibility, we recolor a vertex to the color of an adjacent vertex, creating a new equal edge. This is exactly forward copy operation! Wait reverse operation recolors u to d; if d equals neighbor w's color, then u becomes equal to w. This is like copying color from w to u (forward operation) in reverse? Actually forward operation copies from w to u, making them equal. Reverse operation can also make them equal by recoloring u to w's color. So the reverse process can perform forward copy operations too, but also can recolor to arbitrary colors that may not match neighbor (freezing). To maintain ability, we mostly recolor to neighbor colors, which is forward copy. Interesting.

Maybe the set of reachable arrays is exactly those obtainable by a sequence of "copy" operations where we never need to create isolated new colors except at final? But final A may have isolated colors; we can create them at the last step from a flexible edge, then freeze.

The old domino c at (u,v) broken; v may have no c neighbor. So flexibility moved to d at u-w. Let's search memory of AtCoder editorial. I think there is a problem "AGC 016 C +/-" no. Maybe "AGC 007 D Shik and Game"? no.

Let's derive from first principles.

We have a path graph power G with vertices 1..N, edges between distance<=K. Operation: copy color from j to i. This is equivalent to: For any color c, if S_c is set of vertices with color c, then we can add any vertex adjacent to S_c to S_c (by copying c to it), and remove any vertex from S_c (by overwriting it with another color). The final sets S_c^B must be produced. This is a multi-set expansion/contraction process.

Because G is connected, any color c can expand to all vertices if not overwritten. The only issue is that expanding c overwrites vertices of other colors, potentially removing them. But if a color d is needed later, we can expand d before it is overwritten. This suggests a greedy algorithm: process colors in an order such that when we expand color c to its final positions, we don't destroy the last occurrence of any color not yet processed. Since we can choose to expand c only after all colors that need to use sources in the region have been processed? Let's formalize.

Suppose we process colors one by one. For each color c, at its processing time, we ensure all positions that should be c in final are set to c. After processing c, c is no longer needed, so any remaining c copies can be overwritten by later colors. Before processing c, we need at least one c source. Initially sources are A positions with c, plus maybe copies made earlier? Earlier processed colors might have overwritten some c sources; but we can avoid overwriting all c sources until c processed. So we need an ordering of colors such that for each color c, before processing, there is at least one position with c that hasn't been overwritten by previously processed colors. After processing c, we may overwrite all c sources except final positions? Actually final positions of c are set, so c remains there; later colors must not overwrite final positions. So final positions are protected. Thus we can process colors in some order, and when processing c, we set all final c positions to c. Later colors won't touch them. Therefore at the end, c remains.

Can we always process colors in any order if we first propagate each color to its final positions? To propagate c to its final positions, we need a path from a c source to each final position. The path may go through positions that are final for later colors; but we can overwrite them temporarily and restore later. It may go through the last source of a later color; that would destroy it. But we can first duplicate that later color elsewhere, perhaps by processing it earlier? This is circular.

Maybe there is a concept of "safe positions" for each color: final positions of that color. If a color c has at least one final position, then after processing c, that final position is a permanent source of c. Before processing c, we need some source. If all initial sources of c are overwritten by earlier colors, impossible. But we can choose order to avoid.

This is similar to "pebble game" where each color needs a source until its final positions are created. Since final positions are protected, once a color is processed, it is safe. Initially, colors have sources at A positions. We can think of "unprocessed" colors needing at least one source. When we process a color, we can overwrite its sources (except final positions) and it becomes safe. The operation of processing color c: choose a current source of c, propagate c to all final positions of c. This may overwrite sources of unprocessed colors. To avoid losing them, we need ensure each unprocessed color has at least one source outside the set of positions overwritten by this propagation. But we can choose propagation paths to avoid sources? Maybe not.

Maybe there is an ordering from "leaves" of dependency graph. Let's define for each color c, the set of positions that must be overwritten to propagate c to its final positions. If that set contains all sources of another color d, then d must be processed before c. This gives a partial order. If acyclic, possible. This might be the key.

Maybe there is a simpler invariant: For each color c, consider the interval between its leftmost and rightmost occurrence in A? Since graph is path power, to move a color from a source to a final position, it must traverse positions. If the traversal crosses the "last source" of another color, that other color must be processed first. This yields dependencies.

Let's define for each color c, its "territory" maybe the convex hull of A_c and B_c. In 1D, to propagate c from any source in A_c to any target in B_c, the path lies within the interval [min(A_c ∪ B_c), max(A_c ∪ B_c)]. It may overwrite positions in that interval. If another color d has all its A sources inside that interval and none of its B targets? Wait if d unprocessed and all its sources are inside the interval that c must traverse, then processing c before d would destroy d. Thus d must be processed before c. This is reminiscent of interval orders.

For K=1, operation copies adjacent, so to move a value from p to q, you must copy through all intermediate positions. If an intermediate position is the only source of another value, that value must be moved/processed first. For general K, you can jump up to K, so need cover intervals with steps <=K; but still paths.

Wait after processing a value, its final positions become protected and can serve as sources? Actually after processing v, v is safe; later processing of other colors should not overwrite final B_v. But we can process colors in an order such that no later color needs to overwrite earlier processed final positions. Is that always possible? Maybe we can choose paths avoiding final positions? Not necessarily; graph connected, path may need to cross. But if a later color needs to cross an earlier final position, it would overwrite it, violating final. However we could restore it using its source (the earlier color's final positions) after. But that means earlier color is processed and has final source, so can be restored. So overwriting final positions of processed colors is allowed if we can restore them. This complicates but also gives flexibility: processed colors are safe and can be used to restore.

This is more general: We can process positions in some order; when processing i, its source j must currently have value B_i. This can be either an initial source (A_j = B_i) that hasn't been changed, or a previously processed position j with B_j = B_i (because after processing j, it has B_j). This is like building B by starting with positions where A_i = B_i as "active" sources, and repeatedly activating a position i if there is an active position j within K with value B_i. Let's test.

Forward process: We want final B. We can think of "activating" positions to their final value. Initially, any position i with A_i = B_i is already correct and can serve as source of B_i. Also, any position i with A_i != B_i but A_i = B_i? no. But what about initial A positions that have value v but final B_i != v; they are not active for v in final sense, but they can be used as temporary sources to create copies of v, which may later be overwritten. In the activation model, if we only use final-correct positions as sources, we might miss transformations that require using an initial occurrence of v at a position whose final is not v to seed propagation, then later overwrite it. Example A=[1,2,1], B=[2,1,1]. Initial correct positions: pos3 A3=1=B3 active for1. Need activate pos2 (B2=1) using pos3 within distance1 -> activate pos2. Need activate pos1 (B1=2). Indeed we used initial A2=2 as source to set pos1=2, then overwrote pos2 to1. So initial incorrect positions can serve as sources before being overwritten. Thus need include initial positions as temporary sources, not just final-correct.

So we need model with both initial sources and activated final sources. A position i can serve as source of value A_i until it is overwritten (processed). It can also serve as source of value B_i after processed. Thus each position has two possible "states": initial value A_i (available until processed) and final value B_i (available after processed). We need process all positions (overwrite) exactly once? Positions with A_i=B_i can be considered processed at time 0 (available final) or never need overwrite; but their initial value same as final, so available throughout. For positions with A_i != B_i, before processing they provide A_i; after processing they provide B_i and A_i is lost. We need an ordering of processing changed positions such that when processing i, there is some position j within distance K that currently provides B_i (either unprocessed j with A_j = B_i, or processed j with B_j = B_i). At the end all changed positions processed; unchanged positions provide both A and B (same).

The model captures resource loss: processing a position removes its initial value and adds its final value.

This is reminiscent of "graph searching with tokens" where each position has an initial token A_i and final token B_i; operation processes a vertex, consuming its initial token and producing its final token, provided the final token is available from a neighbor's current token (initial if unprocessed, final if processed). We need process all changed vertices. Unchanged vertices have initial=final token available forever.

Now, how to decide if such an ordering exists efficiently for N up to 250000, K up to N?

This is a scheduling problem on a path power graph. Each vertex i has:
- initial token a_i = A_i, available while i unprocessed.
- final token b_i = B_i, available after i processed.
If a_i = b_i, vertex is initially processed? Actually it can be considered processed at start, providing b_i forever and no need to consume a_i. But if we process it, no change; we can mark as processed initially. It still provides b_i. It does not consume anything. So we can treat unchanged vertices as already active with color b_i.

For changed vertices, processing requires that color b_i is currently available at some vertex j within distance K. Availability:
- If j is unprocessed and A_j = b_i, then available.
- If j is processed (including unchanged) and B_j = b_i, then available.
After processing i, availability changes: A_i is no longer available (unless A_i=B_i? but changed so no), B_i becomes available at i.

We need process all changed vertices. This is exactly a "dependency" problem. We can model as a directed graph of dependencies between changed vertices? Let's define for each changed vertex i, to process it we need a source j within K such that either:
- j is unchanged and B_j = b_i (also A_j=b_i), or
- j is changed and (if unprocessed at that time, A_j=b_i; if processed, B_j=b_i).

Since j's availability changes from A_j to B_j upon processing. Thus a changed vertex j can provide color b_i either before processing if A_j=b_i, or after processing if B_j=b_i. It cannot provide both unless A_j=B_j (but then unchanged). So for each ordered pair (i,j) with |i-j|<=K, j can serve as source for i at some time depending on whether j is processed before or after i:
- If j is processed before i, then j provides B_j; so need B_j = b_i.
- If j is processed after i, then j provides A_j; so need A_j = b_i.
- If j is unchanged, it provides A_j=B_j=b_i always.

Thus the relative order between i and j determines which value j can supply. This is like a 2-SAT / topological ordering constraint: For each changed vertex i, there must exist at least one neighbor j (within K) such that either (j processed before i and B_j = b_i) or (j processed after i and A_j = b_i) or j unchanged with A_j=b_i. We need find a total order of changed vertices satisfying these existential constraints.

This is a known problem: "graph ordering with local availability" can be solved by greedy deletion? Since constraints are monotone: processing a vertex removes its initial color and adds its final color. We can think of the set of currently available colors at positions. Initially, available colors are A_i for all i (since all unprocessed), but for unchanged also B_i. Actually before any processing, all A_i are available. So initially, a changed vertex i can be processed if there exists neighbor j with A_j = b_i. This is the first condition. If no such neighbor, it cannot be first. After processing some vertices, their A colors are removed and B colors added. We need process all.

This is similar to "topological sorting with resources" where each vertex consumes its initial resource and produces final resource. We can greedily process any vertex whose required final color is currently available within distance K. If at any point no changed vertex can be processed but some remain, impossible. Is greedy (arbitrary order) complete? For such monotone systems, if there exists a valid order, then repeatedly processing any currently processable vertex should not make the problem harder? Need check. Processing a vertex removes its initial color A_i, which might be needed by other unprocessed vertices. If we process it too early, we might lose a needed resource. Example N=2 swap: Initially, pos1 needs2, pos2 needs1. Both processable? pos1 needs2: neighbor pos2 has A=2 yes. pos2 needs1: neighbor pos1 has A=1 yes. If greedy processes pos1 first, then pos2 stuck. But there is no valid order anyway. Need a case where one order fails but another succeeds. A=[1,2,1], B=[2,1,1]: Initially pos1 needs2 (source pos2 A=2) processable; pos2 needs1 (source pos1 A=1 or pos3 A=1) processable. If greedy processes pos2 first? pos2 needs1, source pos1 A=1. Process pos2 -> pos2 provides1, pos1 still unprocessed provides1. Then pos1 needs2: sources of2? pos2 processed B=1, no; pos2 unprocessed A=2 lost; no other 2. Stuck. But valid order is pos1 then pos2. So arbitrary greedy fails. Need choose order carefully.

This is like we must avoid destroying resources needed by others. We need find an ordering, possibly via dependency constraints.

Let's model as directed graph of "must be before" constraints. If vertex i needs color b_i from vertex j:
- If j can provide b_i only before processing (A_j=b_i, B_j != b_i), then i must be processed before j. (Because after j processed, A_j gone.)
- If j can provide b_i only after processing (B_j=b_i, A_j != b_i), then i must be processed after j.
- If both (A_j=B_j=b_i, unchanged), no constraint.
- If neither, j cannot source i.

For each changed vertex i, we need choose at least one neighbor j that can source b_i, and then impose the corresponding order constraint (i before j or j before i). We need find an assignment of sources and a topological order satisfying all constraints. This is a kind of constraint satisfaction with choices. Maybe can be solved by considering "available sources" and using SCC? Let's see.

If for every changed vertex i, there is at least one neighbor j with either A_j=b_i or B_j=b_i (or both), then maybe we can choose constraints to avoid cycles? Not always N=2 swap: pos1 needs2: neighbor pos2 has A=2, B=1 -> only before, constraint pos1 < pos2. pos2 needs1: neighbor pos1 has A=1, B=2 -> only before, constraint pos2 < pos1. Cycle, impossible. A=[1,2,1] to B=[2,1,1]: pos1 needs2: neighbor pos2 A=2 -> constraint pos1 < pos2. pos2 needs1: neighbors pos1 A=1 (constraint pos2 < pos1) or pos3 A=1=B=1 unchanged (no constraint). Choose pos3, no cycle. So choice matters.

This is reminiscent of "each vertex must choose a predecessor/successor" and can be solved by 2-SAT? But N up to 250000, values up to N, K large. Need efficient.

Maybe there is a simpler characterization in terms of "for each value v, the positions with B_i=v must be reachable from positions with A_i=v in a directed graph where edges go from source to destination with length<=K and sources are consumed?".

Let's think of the process in terms of values rather than positions. For each value v, the set of positions that ever provide v are:
- Initial positions with A_i = v (available until processed).
- Final positions with B_i = v (available after processed).
A position i can provide v if either A_i=v (before processing) or B_i=v (after processing). If A_i=v and B_i=w≠v, then i provides v only before processing. If A_i=w and B_i=v, provides v only after processing. If A_i=B_i=v, provides v always.

Processing position i requires v=B_i available within distance K. This is like each position i needs to be "activated" by an adjacent provider of its final value. After activation, i becomes a provider of its final value (and stops providing its initial value). This is exactly a graph search where vertices have two labels: input label (available initially) and output label (available after activation). We need activate all vertices with input label != output label. Vertices with equal labels are initially active.

This is known as "graph bootstrap percolation" or "target set selection" with two states. There is a known greedy algorithm for "graph cleaning" if the graph is a path? Maybe can use queue and process vertices whose output label is available, but we saw arbitrary order can fail. However maybe if we process all currently processable vertices in a specific order (e.g., using queue) and if stuck, No, is it complete? Counterexample A=[1,2,1], B=[2,1,1]: Initially processable: pos1 (needs2, source pos2), pos2 (needs1, source pos1 or pos3). If queue processes pos2 first, fails, but a valid order exists. So simple queue not complete. But maybe if we process all processable vertices simultaneously? If process pos1 and pos2 simultaneously? Can we? Operation sequential. If process pos1 first, pos2 still processable via pos3. If process pos2 first, pos1 not processable. So order matters. Need choose safe order.

This resembles "dependency graph" where if a vertex i can be sourced by an unchanged/always provider, it is safe to process anytime. If all sources are "before" constraints (i before j), then i must be before j. If all sources are "after" constraints (j before i), then i must be after j. We need find a topological ordering.

Maybe we can construct a directed graph of "forced" dependencies and then check acyclicity, with choices to avoid cycles. Let's define for each changed vertex i, the set of possible sources S_i = {j within K | A_j = b_i or B_j = b_i}. If S_i empty, impossible. For each j in S_i:
- If A_j = b_i and B_j != b_i: choosing j forces i before j (i -> j? Let's define edge i -> j meaning i must precede j).
- If B_j = b_i and A_j != b_i: choosing j forces j before i (edge j -> i).
- If both (A_j=B_j=b_i): no constraint (j is always available).
- If A_j=b_i and B_j=b_i? same.
If a vertex i has at least one always-available source, it can be processed at any time without imposing constraints. Otherwise, it must choose either a before-source (i before j) or an after-source (j before i).

We need choose one source for each i such that the resulting directed graph (with edges representing precedence) is acyclic. This is like finding a feedback-free orientation of choices.

Can we always choose sources to avoid cycles if possible by selecting "always" sources or sources that point to already processed? Maybe we can use topological sort with "available" set: A vertex i is available to process if it has an always-available source OR a source j that has already been processed and B_j=b_i (after-source) OR a source j that is unprocessed and A_j=b_i (before-source). But if we process i using a before-source j, we must ensure j is processed after i, so j remains unprocessed at that time. That's okay if j is currently unprocessed. If we process i using an after-source j, j must already be processed. Thus at any state, a vertex i is processable if there exists neighbor j such that:
- j is processed and B_j = b_i, or
- j is unprocessed and A_j = b_i.
This is exactly the original condition. We need find an order. This is analogous to "topological sorting with two types of edges": An unprocessed vertex j with A_j=b_i acts as a "predecessor" resource that will disappear when j processed, so i must be before j. A processed vertex j with B_j=b_i acts as a "successor" resource, so i must be after j.

This is similar to "DAG constraints with choices" but maybe can be solved greedily by always processing a vertex that has an always-available source or a source that is "safe" (unprocessed with A_j=b_i and j has another source?).

Let's look for known algorithms. The process can be seen as a game on a directed graph where each vertex has initial color and final color. We want to know if there is an ordering. For each changed vertex i, choose a source j. The precedence constraints are of the form i < j or j < i. We need a total order satisfying selected constraints. This is equivalent to finding an assignment of each i to a source such that the directed graph has no cycle. Since each vertex has outdegree at most 1? Actually if we choose one source per i, each changed vertex i has one outgoing edge (i -> j if i before j, or j -> i if j before i). Wait if choose after-source j, edge j -> i. So each changed vertex i is incident to one selected edge, either outgoing from i or incoming from j. The graph has N_changed edges (one per changed vertex), but edge direction depends on source. It may have cycles. We need acyclic.

This is like "functional graph" with edges chosen from allowed pairs. A directed graph where each vertex has outdegree 1 if we orient all selected edges from predecessor to successor? Let's define for each changed vertex i, selected source j. If i must precede j, edge i->j. If j must precede i, edge j->i. So each changed vertex i contributes one edge, but the tail could be i or j. Thus outdegree not uniform. A cycle in this graph means a set of vertices where each needs a resource from next before it can be processed, impossible.

Can we always choose sources to avoid cycles by picking sources that are "later" in some topological order? This is reminiscent of "matroid intersection"? Maybe not.

This is exactly dependency acyclicity. Let's model dependencies between positions/values more concretely.

Consider each changed position i with A_i=x, B_i=y. It "converts" x to y. To perform conversion, y must be available nearby. If y is available at some position j that is also changed (A_j=y, B_j=z), then i must be converted before j (because j's y disappears when j converts to z). If y is available at some position j that will be converted to y (A_j=w, B_j=y), then i must be converted after j (because j provides y only after conversion). If j is unchanged (A_j=B_j=y), no constraint. If j is initially y and finally y? unchanged.

Thus each changed position i needs a source j. The source j can be:
- a "before" source if A_j = y and B_j != y: constraint i before j.
- an "after" source if B_j = y and A_j != y: constraint j before i.
- an "always" source if A_j = B_j = y: no constraint.

This is like we can choose to change active positions to target values, and if target value matches a neighbor, the position remains active; otherwise it becomes inactive. Let's model reverse process as "edge token" with color. A monochromatic edge of color c at (u,v) allows changing u to any color. If we change u to color d of a neighbor w, the edge moves to (u,w) with color d. If we change u to a color not present in neighbors, the edge disappears (unless v has another c neighbor). Thus to preserve an edge, each recolor should be to a neighbor's color. This is exactly forward copy operation: moving the edge is copying a neighbor's color to one endpoint. So reverse process can simulate forward process while maintaining at least one edge. To reach A, we need perform a sequence of forward copies from B to A? Thus if we restrict reverse operations to those that preserve an edge, they are exactly forward operations. The original forward problem A->B is same. So no simplification.

So maybe the one-process-per-position model is correct for all reachable transformations. Let's try to prove: Any operation that temporarily changes a position away from its final value and later back can be eliminated or reordered so each position changes at most once. Since final B is the goal, we can consider the last time each position is changed. At that last change, it is set to B_i. Before that, it may have been used as a source of some other value. But if it was used as a source of value v before its last change, that means at that time it had v. If v != B_i, then it must have been set to v earlier. Could we instead use the original source of v directly to accomplish the same effect, bypassing this position? Not always, due to distance. But maybe the last-change order and source dependencies form a graph where each position's final value is sourced from a position that either initially has it or has already been finally set to it. Temporary values are not needed because any value that passes through a temporary position ultimately originates from some initial source; we can propagate directly from that source to the destination if the temporary path is within K? The temporary path may be needed to bridge distance. But if a position is temporary, it is not final v; after it is overwritten, it becomes something else. In the final sequence, the value v at destination i was copied from temp x. The value v at temp x was copied from some source y. Thus there is a chain of copies of v from initial source to i. The intermediate positions in this chain may not have final v. In the at-most-once model, we allow a position to be processed to its final value and then serve as source for v only if its final value is v. If its final value is not v, it cannot serve in the chain. But maybe we can choose a different chain using only positions whose final value is v or initial sources? Is that always possible if the transformation is reachable? Not necessarily: To move v across a region where no position final v and no initial v within K, you need temporary carriers. But those temporary carriers would have to be overwritten, and their final values restored. This is like routing v through "empty" space. In 1D, if there is a gap of length >K between initial/final v positions, you cannot cross without temporary carriers. But if you have temporary carriers, you can cross the gap, but then you need to restore them. Is that ever possible without losing their values? It requires backup sources of the carriers' final values on the other side, leading to a chain. This chain might correspond to a cycle of values, which could be impossible unless there is a stable source. The at-most-once model might capture impossibility via dependency cycles.

Let's derive the scheduling problem and see if it has a simple solution.

We have positions 1..N, graph edges between distance<=K. Each position i has initial color a_i and final color b_i. If a_i = b_i, position is "fixed" and can be considered processed at start (provides b_i forever, never consumes). If a_i != b_i, position i is a "task" that must be processed exactly once. Processing i requires that color b_i is currently present at some position j with |i-j|<=K. Current presence:
- If j is unprocessed, it provides a_j.
- If j is processed, it provides b_j.
After processing i, i provides b_i and no longer provides a_i.

We need process all tasks. This is a known problem: "graph searching with colored tokens" can be solved by reducing to 2-SAT? Let's examine constraints.

For each task i, define two possible types of sources:
- A-source: an unprocessed position j with a_j = b_i. If we use j, then i must be processed before j (if j is a task) or no constraint if j fixed.
- B-source: a processed position j with b_j = b_i. If we use j, then i must be processed after j (if j is a task) or no constraint if j fixed.

Thus each task i needs at least one source. This is like each task i must be placed in a topological order such that either it has a predecessor task j with b_j=b_i (processed before) or a successor task j with a_j=b_i (processed after) or a fixed source.

This is exactly the condition for a directed graph where each task chooses an incoming edge from a predecessor with same final color or an outgoing edge to a successor with same initial color. We need acyclic.

Can we solve this by considering strongly connected components of a graph where edges are possible "before" constraints? Let's define two directed graphs:
- For each task i, if there is a task j with a_j = b_i and |i-j|<=K, then i can be before j (edge i -> j).
- If there is a task j with b_j = b_i and |i-j|<=K, then i can be after j (edge j -> i).
Fixed positions are sources/sinks with no constraints.

We need select for each i one incoming or outgoing edge such that graph acyclic. This is like finding a topological ordering where each vertex has at least one "support" from earlier or later. Equivalently, an ordering is valid iff for every task i, there exists a task j such that:
- if j is before i, then b_j = b_i and |i-j|<=K;
- if j is after i, then a_j = b_i and |i-j|<=K.
This is a property of the ordering itself, not requiring explicit source assignment. So we can search for an ordering directly.

This resembles "topological sort with local constraints" and can be solved by greedy removal of vertices that are "sources" or "sinks" in the remaining graph. In such scheduling problems, if there is a valid order, then there is a vertex that can be first or last. Let's characterize vertices that can be processed first/last.

A task i can be processed first if it has a source j that is fixed or will be processed after i (a_j=b_i). Since all other tasks unprocessed, any j with a_j=b_i works. So i can be first if there exists j within K with a_j = b_i. This is the initial condition. In N=2 swap, both tasks can be first (pos1 needs2 from pos2, pos2 needs1 from pos1). So first availability not enough.

A task i can be processed last if it has a source j that is fixed or processed before i (b_j=b_i). Since all other tasks processed, any j with b_j=b_i works. So i can be last if there exists j within K with b_j = b_i. In N=2 swap, B=[2,1], no equal adjacent, so neither can be last? Actually b_1=2, no other b=2; b_2=1, no other b=1. Thus no last task. In A=[1,2,1], B=[2,1,1], tasks pos1 (b=2) no other b=2, cannot be last; pos2 (b=1) has b_3=1 fixed, can be last. Valid order pos1 then pos2. So last task must have a final-color neighbor.

This suggests a recursive algorithm: In any valid order, the last processed task must have a neighbor (within K) whose final color equals its final color (either fixed or processed earlier). Because at the last step, all other tasks are processed, so only final colors are available. Thus if no task has a final-color neighbor, and not all tasks processed, impossible. We can remove such a task? Actually the last task must be one with b_i appearing at a neighbor j with b_j=b_i. If we identify a task that can be last, we can process it last and remove it from consideration. But removing it changes the set of processed tasks for the remaining prefix: For the remaining tasks, the removed task will be processed after them, so it provides its initial color a_i during their processing (since unprocessed until last). Thus when considering the prefix, the removed task should be treated as an unprocessed source with color a_i, not as a processed source. Also, since it is last, it needs a final-color source from the prefix (or fixed) to be processed at the end; that source must remain.

This is like reverse deletion: We can build the order from last to first. At each step, choose a task that can be last among the remaining tasks, given that already removed tasks (which will be processed later) are still unprocessed for the remaining prefix? Wait if we build from last to first, when we remove the last task L, for the remaining tasks (processed before L), L is unprocessed, so it provides a_L. Thus L can serve as an A-source for remaining tasks. Also, L's requirement to be processed last must be satisfied by some remaining task j with b_j = b_L (since L uses a processed source). So when choosing L, we need:
- There exists a remaining task/fixed position j with b_j = b_L and |L-j|<=K. (Because at the time L is processed, all remaining tasks are processed, so their final colors available.)
After removing L, we add L as an available A-source with color a_L for the earlier tasks.

This is analogous to the forward process but in reverse. We can greedily remove any task that has a final-color neighbor? Let's test N=2 swap: B=[2,1], tasks pos1 b=2 no final-color neighbor, pos2 b=1 no final-color neighbor, so cannot remove any -> No. A=[1,2,1], B=[2,1,1]: tasks pos1 b=2 no final-color neighbor (pos? b=2 none), pos2 b=1 has fixed pos3 b=1 neighbor, so remove pos2 last. Remaining task pos1. Now removed pos2 provides a_2=2 as A-source for earlier. pos1 needs b_1=2; it has source pos2 (unprocessed from perspective) with a=2 within distance1, so can be removed next. Valid. A=[1,2,3], B=[3,3,3]: tasks pos1 b=3, pos2 b=3. Fixed pos3 b=3. Both have final-color neighbor (pos3 or each other). Remove one last, say pos2. Remaining pos1. Removed pos2 provides a_2=2. pos1 needs3; fixed pos3 b=3 neighbor, so remove. Valid. Order pos1 first, pos2 last? Wait if remove pos2 last, then remaining pos1 processed before pos2. But forward order was pos2 first, pos1 second, so pos2 is not last. Our reverse removal chose pos2 as last, but is there a valid order with pos2 last? Let's check forward A=[1,2,3] to B=[3,3,3]. If pos2 last, process pos1 first? pos1 needs3, source pos3 fixed distance2>K? pos3 to pos1 distance2>K, no. So pos1 cannot be first. Thus pos2 cannot be last. The remaining pos1 needs 3; it has fixed pos3 distance2>K, no. So no valid order with pos2 last. The valid order has pos1 last? Let's see forward order pos2 first, pos1 second, so pos1 is last. Reverse removal should remove pos1 last. pos1 b=3 has final-color neighbor pos2 (b=3) within1, so can be last. Remove pos1. Remaining pos2. Removed pos1 provides a=1. pos2 needs3; fixed pos3 b=3 neighbor, so remove. Valid. So we need choose the correct last task; not any.

Thus the reverse greedy must choose a task that can be last AND after removing it, the remaining tasks are feasible. This is recursive. We need an efficient way.

This is exactly the same as the original problem but with roles swapped? Let's define a state with a set of unprocessed tasks; fixed positions always processed. Initially all tasks unprocessed. We want to remove tasks one by one (from last to first). A task i can be removed if there exists a processed/fixed position j with b_j = b_i within K? Wait at the time i is processed (last among remaining), all other remaining tasks are processed, so their b colors available. Removed tasks (later) are unprocessed, so their a colors available, not b. Thus for removal condition, "processed" set consists of fixed positions plus tasks that have already been removed? No, if we build from last to first, tasks removed earlier are later in order, so they are unprocessed at the time of the current task. Tasks not yet removed are earlier, so they are processed at the time of current task. Let's clarify:

We build order from last to first. Initially no tasks removed; all tasks will be in order. We choose the last task L. At time L is processed, all other tasks are processed. So L needs a source j with b_j = b_L among all other tasks/fixed positions. Thus condition: exists j≠L with b_j=b_L and |L-j|<=K. After choosing L, for the remaining tasks (which are processed before L), L is unprocessed, so it provides a_L. So in the subproblem, L acts like an unprocessed source with color a_L, not a task. We can think of it as a "fixed" source with color a_L? But it will be processed later, so it remains unprocessed throughout the prefix, thus its color a_L is always available. So yes, for the prefix, L is a permanent source of color a_L (like a fixed position with A=B=a_L). It does not need to be processed in the prefix.

Thus the reverse process: Start with all tasks unprocessed. We have a set of permanent sources: fixed positions (A=B) and removed tasks (providing their initial colors). A task i can be removed (processed last among remaining) if there is a permanent source or a remaining task j with b_j = b_i within K? Wait at the time i is processed, all remaining tasks are processed, so their final colors b_j are available. Removed tasks are unprocessed, so their initial colors a_j are available, not b_j. Fixed positions have b=a. Therefore the available colors for i's requirement are:
- For any remaining task j (including fixed? fixed are not tasks), b_j.
- For any removed task j, a_j.
- For fixed positions, b_j = a_j.
But removed tasks are exactly those that will be processed after i, so they are unprocessed and provide a_j. Thus when deciding if i can be removed next, we need a neighbor j such that:
- if j is a remaining task or fixed, b_j = b_i;
- if j is already removed, a_j = b_i.
This is dual to the forward condition. This is getting complicated but maybe symmetric.

Maybe there is a much simpler solution using "topological sort on a graph of positions with edges based on A and B".

Let's define a directed graph D on positions (tasks) as follows: For each task i, add an edge i -> j if |i-j|<=K and A_j = B_i. This means i can be processed before j (using j's initial value). Also add edge j -> i if |i-j|<=K and B_j = B_i. This means i can be processed after j (using j's final value). We need find an ordering where each task i has either an outgoing edge to a later task or an incoming edge from an earlier task (or both). Fixed positions are always available.

This is equivalent to: In the ordering, each task must have at least one "support" edge connecting it to another task/fixed position, with direction consistent with order. This is like every vertex in a DAG must have indegree or outdegree? Actually if we orient edges from earlier to later, then:
- If i uses j's initial value, i must be before j, so edge i -> j (i earlier, j later). Thus i has outgoing edge to a later vertex.
- If i uses j's final value, j must be before i, so edge j -> i (j earlier, i later). Thus i has incoming edge from an earlier vertex.
So in the final DAG (order), each task must have at least one incident edge (incoming or outgoing) to a vertex that is earlier/later accordingly. Fixed positions are always earlier? They are processed at start, so they can only be sources of final values (B) and initial values (same). For a fixed position f, it can serve as source for i if B_f = B_i (always available), so edge f -> i (f earlier). It can also serve as initial source if A_f = B_i, but since A_f=B_f, same. So fixed positions are sources only.

Thus we need a total order of tasks such that for each task i, there exists a neighbor j with:
- either j is fixed and B_j = B_i (then j before i), or
- j is a task and ( (B_j = B_i and j before i) or (A_j = B_i and i before j) ).

This is a "graph ordering" problem. Could be solved by checking if the directed graph of "must be before" constraints has a cycle when we choose all possible edges? Let's define two types of edges:
- Type F (final): if B_j = B_i and |i-j|<=K, then j can be before i. This is a potential edge j -> i.
- Type I (initial): if A_j = B_i and |i-j|<=K, then i can be before j. This is a potential edge i -> j.

For each task i, it needs at least one incident potential edge (incoming Type F from some j, or outgoing Type I to some j). If a task has no incident potential edge, impossible.

Now, if we take the directed graph G containing all Type F edges (j -> i) and all Type I edges (i -> j), and contract strongly connected components? In any valid order, edges must go from earlier to later. If there is a cycle in G consisting of edges that are all "required" (i.e., the only possible support for each vertex), then impossible. But choices can avoid cycles. This is like finding a subgraph of G with one incident edge per vertex that is acyclic. This is equivalent to the graph G having no directed cycle in every possible? Actually we can choose a subset of edges such that each vertex has indegree+outdegree>=1 and the subgraph is acyclic. Since we can always add edges from order, we just need an acyclic orientation of a subset. This is possible iff the underlying directed graph G has no directed cycle that is "closed" under choices? If G has a directed cycle v1->v2->...->vk->v1 where each edge is the only support for its tail? Then impossible. But if choices exist, maybe possible. This is like finding a feedback vertex set? Could be NP-hard in general? But graph is a path power and colors small? Need exploit structure.

So the at-most-once model was too restrictive for pos1, but the real obstruction is a cycle of values: 3 needs to cross 4's only final/source, 4 needs to cross 3's sources? Let's analyze.

This suggests a dependency graph between values based on intervals:
- To move value v from its initial positions to its final positions, it must occupy a set of positions (a path). If that set contains a position that is the only current source of value w, then w must be established before v moves there.
- To establish w, it may need to move through positions that are sources of v, creating cycle.

Maybe the problem can be solved by considering the directed graph of values where there is an edge v -> w if the convex hull of A_v ∪ B_v contains a position that is in A_w but not in B_w? Let's define.

Actually if K>1, you can jump, but still the convex hull between source and target is within I_v. It may not need to visit every position, but any position between a source and target that is used as a carrier is in I_v. Let's try to solve the scheduling problem with temporary overwrites using graph of values and intervals, and see if we can derive an algorithm.

Observation: Operation is local but values can propagate arbitrarily. The only way to lose a value is to overwrite its last occurrence. Therefore, to transform A to B, we need ensure that for each value v that appears in B, there is at least one occurrence of v at all times until all B_v positions are set. Once a B_v position is set, it can serve as a permanent source. So we can think of "activating" values: Initially, values with A_v nonempty are active. A value v is "safe" once at least one B_v position has been set (or if A_v=B_v at some position, safe from start). To set a B_v position i, we need an active occurrence of v within distance K. This occurrence can be an initial A_v position (active until overwritten) or a safe B_v position. After setting i, v becomes safer (another source). We need eventually set all B_v positions.

This is like a multi-source BFS where each value v has a set of "active" positions (currently v). Initially active positions are A_v. We can add a position i to active set of v if it is adjacent to an active v position. We can also remove positions from active set of v by overwriting them with other values. To finalize, we need active set of v to include B_v at the end. Since overwriting can remove, but if we never overwrite B_v positions, they remain.

Maybe we can choose a strategy: For each value v, expand v to all its B_v positions, then never overwrite them. Before expanding v, we need ensure that expanding v doesn't destroy the last active occurrence of any value w that hasn't been expanded yet. This is the earlier "process values in order" idea. Is it always optimal to process values one by one, expanding each to its final positions and then leaving them? In sample3, if we process 5 first, then 3, etc., maybe fail due to cycle. But any successful strategy can be transformed into such a value-by-value process? Since final positions are protected, we can schedule expansions of values; when a value is expanded, it may overwrite temporary positions, but we can restore them later. However if we expand all values in some order and never overwrite final positions of earlier values, later values may need to cross them. But they can overwrite and restore earlier values because earlier values have final sources. So value-by-value with restoration is possible if dependencies acyclic.

This is analogous to "graph searching" where each value needs to be "activated" and can be used as a source. Maybe the condition is that the directed graph of values with edges v -> w if the interval of v contains an A_w position that is not B_w? Let's test with sample3.

Let's define for each value v, the set of positions that v must occupy to connect A_v to B_v. In 1D with K=1, the minimal path between any A_v and B_v is the interval between them. To make all B_v, v must at some time occupy a connected set spanning from some A_v to all B_v. This set may be the union of intervals from A_v to each B_v. The "footprint" F_v = convex hull of A_v ∪ B_v. If v expands, it will overwrite all positions in F_v (if K=1) unless there are gaps? Actually to move from A_v to B_v, every intermediate position must be temporarily v. So F_v must be temporarily all v. Thus processing v overwrites all positions in F_v. Therefore, if F_v contains a position x that is the only active source of w (i.e., A_w positions all inside F_v and not yet safe), then w must be processed before v. This gives a dependency: v depends on w? Let's define edge v -> w meaning v must be processed after w (w before v) if F_v contains all A_w? More precisely, if F_v contains a position x with A_x = w and x is not a B_w position (so not safe), then processing v before w would destroy w's source. To be safe, w must be processed before v. Thus edge v -> w (v after w). If there is a cycle, impossible.

We need an order of values. For any two values x,y, consider processing order. If x is before y:
- When x is processed, y unprocessed. x's footprint F_x may overwrite A_y. If F_x contains all A_y, y loses all sources -> invalid. Thus if F_x ⊇ A_y, x cannot be before y; must have y before x.
- If y is before x:
  After y processed, y has active positions B_y (and maybe A_y). When x processed later, F_x may overwrite them. If F_x contains all active positions of y, y loses all sources -> invalid. To be safe, y needs at least one active position outside F_x. The set of active positions of y after processing is B_y ∪ (A_y \ overwritten by other values before x). Since we can avoid overwriting A_y outside F_x, the safe condition is (A_y ∪ B_y) \ F_x nonempty. If A_y∪B_y ⊆ F_x, then y cannot be before x. Thus x must be before y.
So for pair (x,y):
- If F_x ⊇ A_y, then y before x.
- If F_x ⊇ (A_y ∪ B_y), then x before y.
These can both hold, causing cycle.

This is much more complex: For each value, we can choose a subset of A_v sources to connect to B_v targets, forming a forest. The footprint is the union of intervals between chosen sources and targets. We need choose footprints to avoid cycles. This is like finding disjoint paths in an interval graph, maybe solvable by greedy matching.

Maybe the problem can be transformed to a matching problem: Since operation copies values, each final position i with B_i=v must be "covered" by some initial position p with A_p=v. The copy path from p to i is a sequence of positions; if we think of each edge in the path as a copy operation, the path can branch. The set of paths for all final positions forms a directed forest in space-time. The only obstruction is cycles of dependencies between positions. This is equivalent to finding a set of directed paths in the graph G from A_v to B_v for each v, such that the paths are "non-crossing" in a certain sense? In 1D, paths can cross but maybe can be uncrossed.

Let's consider the final assignment of each position i to an initial source p(i) with A_{p(i)} = B_i. However if we assign each final position to an initial source, the copy path may use intermediate positions that are assigned to other sources. If parent is an initial source, it hasn't been overwritten; if parent is a final position of same value, it has been processed. Thus each node's parent is either an initial source (unprocessed) or a processed node with same final value. This is exactly the at-most-once model but with chains: a node can copy from a processed node whose final value equals its value, not necessarily initial. Now, how to solve the scheduling problem efficiently?

We have tasks i with A_i != B_i. For each task i, it can be processed if there exists a neighbor j within K such that:
- j is fixed (A_j=B_j) and B_j = B_i, or
- j is a task and ( (B_j = B_i and j processed before i) or (A_j = B_i and i processed before j) ).

This is equivalent to finding an ordering. This can be solved by a greedy algorithm using a queue of tasks that are "currently processable" in the forward sense, but we saw arbitrary greedy can fail. However maybe if we process tasks in reverse (from last to first), greedy is complete? Let's examine.

Reverse deletion algorithm:
We want to build order from last to first. At any point, remaining tasks are those not yet placed (will be processed earlier). Removed tasks are placed later, so they are unprocessed during the remaining prefix; they provide their initial colors A_j. Fixed positions provide their colors always. A remaining task i can be placed next (i.e., be the last among remaining) if it can be processed at that time in the forward order. At that time, all remaining tasks (including i? i is being processed now) are processed? Wait if i is last among remaining, then all other remaining tasks are processed before i, so they provide final colors B_j. Removed tasks are after i, so unprocessed, provide A_j. Fixed positions provide B_j=A_j. Thus i is processable if there exists neighbor j such that:
- j is remaining (other than i) or fixed, and B_j = B_i; OR
- j is removed, and A_j = B_i.
Because if j remaining, it will be processed before i, so its final color available. If j removed, it is after i, so its initial color available.
This is the reverse condition.

Initially, no tasks removed. A task i can be last (first removed) if there exists a neighbor j (task or fixed) with B_j = B_i. This is the "last task" condition. In A=[1,2,3],B=[3,3,3], tasks pos1,pos2. Initially, pos1 has neighbor pos2 with B=3, so can be last; pos2 has neighbor pos3 fixed B=3, can be last. If we choose pos1 last (remove pos1), then removed pos1 provides A=1. Remaining pos2. Now pos2 can be removed if there exists neighbor j remaining/fixed with B_j=3 (pos3 fixed) OR removed with A_j=1? pos1 removed A=1, but pos2 needs3, so no. pos3 fixed B=3 within1, yes. So remove pos2. Order: pos2 first, pos1 last. Valid. If we choose pos2 last first, removed pos2 provides A=2. Remaining pos1. pos1 needs3; neighbor pos3 fixed B=3 distance2>K, removed pos2 A=2 not3, no other. So stuck. Thus choice matters. But reverse greedy can be made complete by always removing a task that is "safe" (has a final-color source that will remain after removal). Need decide.

This is analogous to topological sorting where we can remove a vertex if it has a "support" from remaining/fixed or removed. We need find any sequence. This is exactly the same as forward, just with roles of A and B swapped? Let's define a new array C where for tasks, initial color = B_i, final color = A_i? Reverse process condition: A task i can be removed if it has a source of color B_i from a remaining task's final color or removed task's initial color. This is like the original problem with A' = B? Not exactly.

Maybe we can solve both directions by a greedy algorithm that maintains a set of "available" tasks and uses a queue, but to avoid wrong choices, we should process tasks that have no choice? In A=[1,2,3],B=[3,3,3], both pos1 and pos2 initially removable; choosing pos2 first failed, pos1 first succeeded. What distinguishes pos1? pos1 has no initial source? Actually pos1 needs3, its only source is pos2 (remaining final) or pos3 fixed (distance2>K). If we remove pos1, it becomes an A-source of1, which is not needed by pos2. If we remove pos2, it becomes A-source of2, not needed by pos1. The successful choice is to remove the task whose initial color is not needed by remaining tasks? More generally, removing a task removes its final color from the remaining set (since it will be unprocessed for earlier tasks) and adds its initial color. For the remaining tasks, the removed task can serve as an initial source of A_removed. Thus removing a task is beneficial if some remaining task needs A_removed; harmful if remaining tasks needed B_removed as a final source. In the example, removing pos2 removes final 3 source needed by pos1; harmful. Removing pos1 removes final 3 source needed by pos2? pos2 also needs3, but pos2 has fixed pos3 source, so not harmful. So we should remove tasks that have alternative final sources.

This suggests a greedy algorithm: Repeatedly remove a task that can be last and whose removal does not destroy the only final-color source for any remaining task. This is like "leaf removal" in a directed graph. We can model dependencies:
- A task i needs at least one final-color source among remaining/fixed (to be last). If we remove i, the remaining tasks lose i's final color B_i as a potential source, but gain i's initial color A_i as a potential source.
This is similar to "pebble game" and can be solved by maintaining counts of final colors among remaining tasks and initial colors among removed tasks.

Let's define for the reverse deletion process:
Remaining tasks set R. Removed tasks set D (processed later). Fixed positions F always available with color B=A.
For a task i in R to be removable, it needs a neighbor j such that:
- j in R∪F and B_j = B_i, OR
- j in D and A_j = B_i.
After removing i, D gains i (with initial color A_i), R loses i (so final color B_i count decreases).

We need remove all tasks. This is a monotone process: R shrinks, D grows. We can maintain for each task i whether it is currently removable. If at any point no removable task but R nonempty, impossible. But arbitrary removal order may fail (as seen). However if we always remove a task that is "safe" in the sense that after removal, the remaining tasks still have at least one removable? This is like finding a valid deletion order; greedy with any removable might fail. Need a strategy.

Maybe the process is confluent if we remove all currently removable tasks simultaneously? In A=[1,2,3],B=[3,3,3], initially removable {pos1,pos2}. If we remove both simultaneously? Can't; order pos2 then pos1? pos2 first fails, but if we remove pos1 first then pos2 succeeds. Simultaneous removal would correspond to order pos1,pos2? Actually if both removed, remaining none, valid order pos2 first, pos1 last? Wait order from reverse removal: remove pos1 last, then pos2 first? Let's map: Remove pos1 (last), then pos2 (first) gives forward order pos2, pos1. Valid. If we remove pos2 first (last), then pos1 (first) gives forward order pos1, pos2, invalid. So not any.

Can we characterize valid orders via a partial order of tasks? In reverse deletion, if task i is removed before task j, then i is later in forward order than j. For i to be removable before j, i needs a final-color source from some remaining task/fixed or initial source from already removed. If i uses j's final color, then j must remain (not removed yet), so j is removed after i, meaning j before i in forward. This is a dependency: i depends on j's final color -> j before i. If i uses an already removed k's initial color, then k is after i in forward, so k after i; no dependency on remaining.

This is exactly the same as the original constraint graph. We need find a topological order. This is a known problem: "graph ordering with local constraints" can be solved by checking if there is a cycle in a directed graph where each vertex has an edge to a "witness". Since each vertex needs at least one witness, we can think of it as a directed graph where each vertex chooses a witness. We need choose witnesses to avoid cycles. Maybe there is a simpler condition: The process is possible iff there is no directed cycle in the graph H where vertices are tasks, and we add an edge i -> j if |i-j|<=K and (A_i = B_j or B_i = B_j)? Let's test.

Consider forward order. If task i is processed before task j, then at the time i is processed, j is unprocessed, so j can provide A_j. Thus if A_j = B_i and |i-j|<=K, i can be before j. This is an edge i -> j (i before j). If task j is processed before i, then j can provide B_j. Thus if B_j = B_i and |i-j|<=K, j before i, edge j -> i. So for each unordered pair (i,j), the possible order depends on colors. If we create a directed graph with all potential edges:
- Edge i -> j if A_j = B_i (i can be before j)
- Edge j -> i if B_j = B_i (j can be before i)
Then a valid order is a topological order of a subgraph where each vertex has at least one incident edge oriented consistently. If the full graph has a directed cycle, is it impossible? Not necessarily, because we can choose not to use some edges. But if a vertex has only one incident edge, it's forced. We can iteratively contract forced edges. This is similar to finding a topological order with "each vertex has a predecessor or successor". There is a known algorithm: repeatedly remove vertices that have no incident edges (impossible), and vertices that are "sources" or "sinks" in the remaining graph? Let's think.

In any valid order, consider the first task in the order. It has no predecessors among tasks, so it must be supported by a successor: there exists j after it with A_j = B_i. Thus the first task must have an outgoing edge i -> j (using j's initial value). Similarly, the last task must have an incoming edge j -> i (using j's final value). Therefore, in the chosen subgraph, the first task has outdegree at least1 and indegree0? It may also have incoming edges from fixed positions? Fixed positions are not tasks; they are always before, so they can be predecessors. If a task has a fixed neighbor with B_f=B_i, it can be first (using fixed as predecessor). In graph terms, fixed positions are sources. So first task can have incoming from fixed.

Now, is the condition "every task has at least one incident edge in full graph" sufficient? No, N=2 swap tasks have edges but cycle no external source. Need SCC condition.

Let's formalize full graph G on tasks:
For each task i, add directed edge i -> j if |i-j|<=K and A_j = B_i. (i can be before j, using j's initial value)
Add directed edge j -> i if |i-j|<=K and B_j = B_i. (j can be before i, using j's final value)
Also fixed positions can be considered as sources: if there is a fixed position f with |i-f|<=K and B_f = B_i, then i has an external source (fixed) meaning it can be first/anytime with predecessor. We cannot topologically order H if it has cycles. However, we don't need to respect all edges; we can choose a subset of edges to break cycles. The SCC condition says we can order SCCs in a topological order of the condensation (which is acyclic), and within each SCC, we need find an order that respects enough edges. Since the SCC is strongly connected, it contains cycles. We cannot topologically order all edges in an SCC. But we don't need to use all edges; we need choose a subset that is acyclic and gives each vertex support. In a strongly connected component, can we choose an acyclic subset where each vertex has support, given an external incoming/outgoing? Yes, take a spanning tree rooted at the external connection. For example, if there is an external incoming edge to root r, then for every vertex v, take a path from r to v in H. But the path may contain cycles; we can take a spanning arborescence (directed spanning tree) from r to all vertices, which exists if S is strongly connected. This arborescence is acyclic and gives every vertex (except root) an incoming edge from its parent. The root has the external incoming edge. Thus every vertex has incoming support. Order vertices by the arborescence (parents before children). This is valid! Similarly, if there is an external outgoing edge from some vertex r to outside, take a spanning arborescence of reversed edges? Since S strongly connected, for every vertex v there is a path from v to r in H. If we order vertices in reverse topological order of this arborescence (children before parents), then every vertex except r has an outgoing edge to its parent (later), and r has outgoing to outside. Thus every vertex has outgoing support. So an SCC with any external incoming or outgoing can be ordered.

If an SCC has both no incoming and no outgoing (isolated), no vertex has external support. Within the SCC, any acyclic subset of edges must have a source vertex (no incoming) and a sink vertex (no outgoing). The source vertex would need an outgoing edge to later (since no incoming), and the sink would need an incoming edge from earlier. In a finite acyclic graph, a source has outgoing if there is at least one edge; a sink has incoming if there is at least one edge. Therefore, in an SCC, an acyclic subset must give each vertex at least one outgoing type A or incoming type B. This is more subtle. If p's support is also incoming type B from its parent, then we need a chain of type B edges from root to v. Thus to support all vertices via incoming edges, the type B edges alone must form a spanning arborescence. If not, some vertices may need outgoing type A support. Similarly, outgoing support requires type A edges.

So the SCC condition might need to consider the two edge types separately. Let's test N=2 swap. H has type A edges pos1->pos2 (A2=2=B1) and pos2->pos1 (A1=1=B2). So an SCC with only type A edges and no external cannot be ordered because type A edges support tails, and in a cycle, the last vertex has no outgoing to later.

If an SCC has a cycle of type A edges, to order it, we need at least one vertex with external outgoing type A or incoming type B? Let's analyze.

We have two types of support:
- Type A edge u -> v (A_v = B_u) supports u if v is after u.
- Type B edge u -> v (B_u = B_v) supports v if u is before v.

Thus type A support points "forward" from the supported vertex to a later vertex. Type B support points "backward" from an earlier vertex to the supported vertex.

In an ordering, consider the first vertex in an SCC. It cannot have type B incoming from within SCC (since no earlier in SCC) unless external. It must have type A outgoing to a later vertex (within SCC or outside). The last vertex cannot have type A outgoing to later within SCC; it must have type B incoming from earlier (within SCC or outside). Therefore, within an SCC, if there is no external connection, the first vertex needs a type A edge to a later vertex, and the last vertex needs a type B edge from an earlier vertex. This is possible even in a cycle of type A? N=2 swap: choose order pos1,pos2. First pos1 has type A edge to pos2 (later) OK. Last pos2 needs type B incoming from pos1? B_pos1=2, B_pos2=1, no. Therefore, for a task v, A_v != B_v. Thus if there is a type A edge u->v, then A_v = B_u. If also type B edge u->v (B_u=B_v), then A_v = B_v, impossible. So between two tasks, type A and type B edges cannot both exist in the same direction? Let's check:
Type A u->v: A_v = B_u.
Type B u->v: B_u = B_v.
If both, then A_v = B_v, so v fixed. Since v task, not both. Thus for task pairs, each directed edge is either type A or type B, not both. Moreover, type A edge u->v implies A_v = B_u, so v's initial value equals u's final value. Type B edge u->v implies B_u = B_v, so u and v have same final value.

So the problem is finding an acyclic subset of edges where each task has at least one outgoing type A or incoming type B. This is equivalent to finding a topological ordering such that each vertex has either a type A edge to a later vertex or a type B edge from an earlier vertex. Let's define:
- A vertex can be first if it has a type A edge to some other vertex (which will be later) OR a type B incoming from a fixed source? Actually if first, it cannot have type B incoming from earlier tasks, but can have fixed source (fixed is earlier). The latter j must be later, but if j is also in remaining, we can order j after i. So i can be first if it has any type A edge to a remaining task or fixed source.
- A vertex can be last if it has a type B incoming from a fixed source or a task that will be earlier, i.e., exists fixed f with B_f=B_i, OR exists task j with B_j=B_i and |i-j|<=K (type B incoming from earlier). It does not need outgoing.

In a valid order, the first vertex must satisfy first condition; the last must satisfy last condition. If we remove a first vertex, the remaining problem is the same? When we remove the first vertex i, for the remaining tasks, i is processed earlier, so it provides its final color B_i as a type B source. Thus we should add i as a "fixed" source with color B_i for the remaining subproblem. Also, i's initial color A_i is no longer available (since processed), so type A edges from remaining tasks to i (using A_i) are no longer valid. This is exactly the reverse of the reverse deletion.

This suggests a greedy algorithm: Repeatedly choose a vertex that can be first, remove it, and add its final color as a permanent source; remove its initial color from availability. If at any point no vertex can be first but tasks remain, impossible. But arbitrary choice may fail? Let's test A=[1,2,3],B=[3,3,3], K=1. Tasks pos1,pos2, fixed pos3=3.

Maybe we can solve using 2-SAT? Let's think of the ordering as a permutation. The support condition for each task i:
- Either there exists a task j after i with A_j = B_i and |i-j|<=K (call this "A-support").
- Or there exists a task j before i with B_j = B_i and |i-j|<=K, or fixed source (call "B-support").
This is a disjunction over possible j of (i before j) or (j before i). This is a 2-SAT-like constraint but with many variables and order comparisons. However, because the graph is a path and K can be large, maybe we can use interval scheduling.

Could be solved by considering the graph of "mandatory" A-edges from vertices that have no B-source? Let's design reverse deletion with forced choices:
We build order from last to first. At each step, choose a task i to be last among remaining. For i to be last, it needs a B-source from a remaining task/fixed (since no later tasks to provide A-source). So i must have a neighbor j (remaining task or fixed) with B_j = B_i. If no such task, i cannot be last. If multiple, choose one. After removing i, i becomes an A-source for earlier tasks (provides A_i). For the remaining tasks, the availability of B_i as a B-source decreases (since i removed), and availability of A_i as an A-source increases.

This is a monotone process. We need determine if there exists a removal sequence. This is exactly the same as the original but with roles swapped and maybe easier because last task condition is local (B-source). We can use a greedy algorithm: always remove a task that has a B-source and whose removal does not destroy the only B-source for another task? This is like "peeling" a directed graph. There is a known algorithm for such problems: maintain for each task the count of potential B-sources among remaining tasks/fixed. A task is removable if count >0. If we remove a task, it decrements B-source counts for tasks that have B_j = B_i and within K, and increments A-source availability for tasks that need A_i. The increment can make new tasks removable? Wait removal of i as last means for earlier tasks, i is unprocessed, so it provides A_i. Thus a remaining task k that needs A_i (A_i = B_k) gains an A-source, but A-source is not relevant for being last; for a task to be removed next (which is second-to-last), it needs a B-source from remaining tasks/fixed, not A-source from removed tasks. Actually in reverse deletion, when removing i (last), the next task k (second-to-last) at its processing time has i unprocessed (later), so i provides A_i, not B_i. But k's requirement to be processed at that time is B_k, which can be satisfied by:
- a remaining task/fixed with B_j = B_k (processed before k), or
- a removed task with A_j = B_k (unprocessed after k).
So removed tasks' A colors can serve as B-sources for remaining tasks! Wait earlier I said for last task, removed tasks provide A, but for the next task, removed tasks are later, so they are unprocessed and provide A. Thus A_i from removed task i can satisfy a remaining task k if A_i = B_k. So removing i increases the "available sources" for tasks needing color A_i. Therefore, a task k that had no B-source among remaining/fixed might become removable after some tasks with A_k are removed. This is the key.

So reverse deletion state:
- Remaining tasks R.
- Removed tasks D (later in order).
A task i in R is removable (can be next last among R) if there exists a neighbor j such that:
  * j in R∪F and B_j = B_i (processed before i), OR
  * j in D and A_j = B_i (unprocessed after i).
After removing i, D gains i with A_i.

This is exactly the same as the forward process but with colors swapped? It is symmetric.

We can maintain for each task i the count of current sources:
source_count[i] = (# of j in R∪F with B_j = B_i and |i-j|<=K) + (# of j in D with A_j = B_i and |i-j|<=K).
If source_count[i] > 0, i is removable. Initially D empty, source_count is B-source count from remaining/fixed. When we remove a task r:
- r leaves R: for any task i with B_i = B_r and |i-r|<=K, source_count[i] decreases by 1 (because r no longer in R providing B_r).
- r enters D: for any task i with B_i = A_r and |i-r|<=K, source_count[i] increases by 1 (because r now in D providing A_r).
We need remove all tasks. This is a dynamic process. Is any order of removals valid? We saw arbitrary removal can fail. Let's consider the "last operation" perspective. In any successful transformation, consider the last operation performed. It changes some position i to B_i. Before this operation, all other positions already have their final values? Actually if it's the last operation, after it all positions final. Now, the theorem: A valid ordering exists iff every task has at least one incident edge (incoming from fixed/task or outgoing to task), and every SCC of the task graph that has no incoming from outside (fixed or other SCC) and no outgoing to outside contains both an A-type edge and a B-type edge? There is a known technique: For each value v, maintain a set of positions with A_j=v and B_j=v. During DFS, to get neighbors of u, we need all unvisited positions in A_list[B_u] and B_list[B_u] within [u-K,u+K]. We can use a DSU "next unvisited" for each list to skip visited nodes. Let's implement Kosaraju:
- Need forward DFS to compute order.
- Need reverse DFS on reversed graph. Reversed edges:
  Original edge u -> v if (A_v = B_u OR B_v = B_u).
  Reverse edge v -> u if (A_v = B_u OR B_v = B_u). From a node x in reverse, we need find all u such that x is v and condition holds: A_x = B_u OR B_x = B_u, and |u-x|<=K. So from x, reverse neighbors are all u within K with B_u = A_x OR B_u = B_x. This is symmetric: reverse adjacency of x uses positions with B_u = A_x or B_u = B_x. Nice.

So for forward DFS from u, query positions with A_pos = B_u or B_pos = B_u within [u-K,u+K].
For reverse DFS from u, query positions with B_pos = A_u or B_pos = B_u within [u-K,u+K].

We need data structures:
- For each value v, a sorted list of positions with A_pos = v (for forward A).
- For each value v, a sorted list of positions with B_pos = v (for forward B and reverse B).
- For each value v, a sorted list of positions with B_pos = v (reverse A? reverse needs B_pos = A_u, so same B_list[A_u]).
Actually reverse needs B_pos = A_u or B_pos = B_u. So only B_lists! Forward needs A_list[B_u] and B_list[B_u]. Thus we need A_lists and B_lists.
